## Problem Understanding

1. Alice has N public addresses with different amounts of satoshi
2. She wants to split her bitcoins so that no address has more than x satoshi
3. Each transaction costs f satoshi (paid from the source address)
4. We need to find the total fee she will pay to achieve this goal

## Key Insights

1. For each address with amount > x, we need to split it into smaller addresses
2. When we transfer money from an address, we pay a fee f (which is deducted from the source)
3. The amount transferred is (current_amount - x) - fee, because after paying the fee, the remaining amount goes to the new address
4. We need to continue splitting until all addresses have at most x satoshi

## Algorithm

For each address with amount `a_i`:
- If `a_i <= x`: no action needed, it's already valid
- If `a_i > x`: we need to split it into multiple addresses
- Each transfer reduces the amount by `f` (fee) and adds `(a_i - f)` to the new address
- We need to keep splitting until the remaining amount is ≤ x

For an address with amount `a_i` where `a_i > x`:
- After first transfer: we have `(a_i - f)` left in original address, and we create a new address with `(a_i - f)` 
- But actually, we transfer some amount to create a new address, and pay fee f

Let me think more carefully:
- We start with amount `a_i`
- We transfer some amount `t` to a new address
- The fee `f` is deducted from the source address
- So the source address ends up with `a_i - t - f`
- The new address gets `t`
- We want both `a_i - t - f ≤ x` and `t ≤ x`

To minimize waste, we want the source address to have as much as possible without exceeding x, so we want `a_i - t - f = x` (just barely under or equal to x).

So: `a_i - t - f = x`
Therefore: `t = a_i - x - f`

But we also need `t ≥ 1` (since we can't transfer 0), and we need `t ≤ x`.

The amount we can transfer is `a_i - x - f` (when possible).

However, if `a_i - x - f < 0`, then we don't need to transfer.

Actually, if `a_i ≤ x`, no transfer needed.

If `a_i > x`, we need to transfer to split it.

For each transfer operation:
- We reduce the amount at source by `f + t` where `t` is the amount transferred
- We create one new address with amount `t`

We want to find how many transfers we need for each original address.

For an address with amount `a_i > x`:
- Initial amount: `a_i`
- After transfer: source has `a_i - f - t`, and we create a new address with `t`
- We want `a_i - f - t ≤ x`, so `t ≥ a_i - x - f`
- To split effectively, we transfer the maximum possible amount `t = a_i - x - f`

Wait, this might not be optimal. Let me reconsider.

Actually, we just need to ensure that after each transfer, no address exceeds x.

For a single address with amount `a_i > x`:
- We need to transfer enough so that the remaining in the original address is ≤ x
- So we transfer at least `a_i - x - f` (since we pay fee f)
- But we can transfer more to create multiple addresses

The key insight is that we need to determine how many times we need to perform transactions on each address.

For each address with amount `a_i > x`:
- We need to keep splitting until the address has at most x satoshi
- Each transaction reduces the current amount by `f` (fee)
- The amount transferred is what goes to the new address

Actually, let's think differently:

For an address with amount `a_i > x`:
- We need to keep transferring until the amount in this address becomes ≤ x
- Each transfer costs `f` (from the source) and the source amount decreases by `f`
- The amount we transfer can be up to `x`, so we split what's left

But the total fee is simply the sum of fees for all transactions.

Each transaction costs `f`, so if we perform `k` transactions, total fee is `k * f`.

We need to determine how many transactions are needed.

For an address with amount `a_i > x`:
- Initially: `a_i`
- After first transaction: source has `a_i - f`, and we create a new address with some amount
- We want to make sure no address has more than x

The minimum number of transfers for one address is the number of times we need to reduce the amount until it's ≤ x.

Each transaction reduces the current amount by `f`, but we also transfer some amount to create new addresses.

Actually, the number of transactions needed for an address with amount `a_i > x` is the number of times we need to split it.

If we have amount `a_i` and we want each piece to have at most `x`, then we need to split until the remaining amount is ≤ x.

But each split costs `f`.

For an address with amount `a_i > x`, the number of transactions needed is:
- We need to keep transferring until the source address has ≤ x
- Each transfer reduces the amount in the source by `f`
- So we need to perform `ceil((a_i - x) / f)` transactions? 

Wait, let me trace through the example:
- Initial: [13, 7, 6], x=6, f=2
- 13 > 6, so we need to split it
- First transaction: transfer 5 from address with 13 to new address
- Source has 13 - 5 - 2 = 6, new address has 5
- Now we have [6, 7, 6, 5]
- 7 > 6, so we need to split it
- Transfer 1 from address with 7 to new address
- Source has 7 - 1 - 2 = 4, new address has 1
- Now we have [6, 4, 6, 5, 1]

So transactions: 2, total fee = 2 * 2 = 4.

For address with 13: we transfer 5, which is 13 - 6 - 2 = 5
For address with 7: we transfer 1, which is 7 - 6 - 2 = 1

In general, for an address with amount `a_i > x`:
- We transfer `(a_i - x - f)` to create a new address
- The remaining in source is `a_i - (a_i - x - f) - f = x`
- This is optimal since we just barely keep the source under x

But we don't necessarily need to transfer exactly this amount. The amount transferred can be less, but the key is that we need to perform transactions until the amount in each address is ≤ x.

The number of transactions for an address with amount `a_i` is:
- If `a_i ≤ x`: 0 transactions
- If `a_i > x`: we need to perform enough transactions so that after reductions, the amount is ≤ x
- Each transaction reduces the current amount by `f` (fee), and we can distribute the remaining amount to new addresses

Actually, since we're keeping track of the total amount, and we only care about the total fee, and each transaction costs `f`, we just need to count how many transactions are performed.

For each address with amount `a_i > x`, how many transactions are needed?

We need to keep transferring until the amount in that address becomes ≤ x.

Each transaction reduces the amount in that address by `f` (the fee).

So if we start with `a_i` and want it to be ≤ `x`, we need to reduce it by at least `a_i - x`.

Since each transaction reduces it by `f`, we need at least `ceil((a_i - x) / f)` transactions.

But wait, after each transaction, the amount in that address becomes `current_amount - f`, not by transferring.

Actually, when we transfer, we pay a fee of `f` from the source address, so the source amount decreases by `f`. The amount transferred is what goes to the new address.

So the amount in the source address decreases by `f` per transaction.

To go from `a_i` to at most `x`, we need to reduce by at least `a_i - x`.

Since each transaction reduces by exactly `f`, we need `ceil((a_i - x) / f)` transactions per address.

Let me verify with the example:
- Address 13: (13-6)/2 = 7/2 = 3.5, ceil = 4
- Address 7: (7-6)/2 = 1/2 = 0.5, ceil = 1
- Total transactions = 4 + 1 = 5, total fee = 5 * 2 = 10, but expected is 4.

This is wrong.

I think I confused myself. Let me re-read the example.

The example shows:
- Start: 13, 7, 6
- Transaction 1: transfer 5 from address with 13, fee 2, so address with 13 becomes 13-5-2=6
- Transaction 2: transfer 1 from address with 7, fee 2, so address with 7 becomes 7-1-2=4

Total fee = 4.

For address 13: we don't need to transfer multiple times. We only transfer once to reduce it from 13 to 6.

For address 7: we transfer once to reduce it from 7 to 4.

The number of transactions for an address with amount `a_i > x` is not `ceil((a_i-x)/f)`, because after one transaction, we can create a new address and the original address can have `a_i - (amount_transferred) - f`.

We only transfer once per address to split it. 

Actually, let's think about it differently. We don't need to split further once the amount is reduced to ≤ x. 

For each address with amount > x, we need exactly one transaction to reduce it to ≤ x? 

In the example, we have:
- 13 > 6, so one transaction
- 7 > 6, so one transaction  
- 6 ≤ 6, so no transaction

Total transactions: 2, total fee: 2 * 2 = 4.

This matches!

So the algorithm is:
1. Count how many addresses have amount > x
2. For each such address, we need exactly one transaction to reduce it to ≤ x
3. Total transactions = number of addresses with amount > x
4. Total fee = (number of addresses with amount > x) * f

But is this always optimal? 

What if we have an address with amount 15, x=6, f=2:
- We could transfer 15 - 6 - 2 = 7 to a new address
- Original becomes 15 - 7 - 2 = 6
- This works in one transaction

What if we have amount 15, x=6, f=4:
- We transfer t to new address
- Original becomes 15 - t - 4 = 11 - t
- We want 11 - t ≤ 6, so t ≥ 5
- We can transfer 5, original becomes 6
- So one transaction suffices

What if we have amount 15, x=6, f=10:
- Original becomes 15 - t - 10 = 5 - t
- We want 5 - t ≤ 6 (always true for t ≥ 0)
- But we also want the transferred amount t ≤ x = 6
- We can transfer any t ≤ 6
- To minimize the amount in source, we want to transfer as much as possible
- But we only need the source to have ≤ 6
- 5 - t ≤ 6 is always true
- So we can transfer 5, source becomes 0
- Or transfer 6, source becomes -1 (invalid)

We need to ensure the source amount is non-negative.

We want to minimize the number of transactions, and it seems one transaction is always sufficient when the initial amount > x, because:
1. We can transfer `max(0, a_i - x - f)` or less
2. The target amount in source becomes `a_i - amount_transferred - f`
3. We need this to be ≤ x and ≥ 0

We can choose amount_transferred such that:
- `a_i - amount_transferred - f ≤ x`
- `a_i - amount_transferred - f ≥ 0`

This gives us: `amount_transferred ≥ a_i - x - f` and `amount_transferred ≤ a_i - f`

As long as `a_i - x - f < a_i - f` (which is true), and as long as `a_i - x - f ≤ a_i - f`, we need `a_i - x - f ≥ 0` to be able to transfer.

Wait, if `a_i - x - f < 0`, then we can't transfer enough to make the source ≤ x? 

Actually, if `a_i ≤ x`, we don't need to transfer. If `a_i > x`, then `a_i - x > 0`, and we need to reduce by at least `a_i - x`.

The fee is `f`, so we need to reduce by `f` per transaction.

After one transaction, the remaining amount is `a_i - f - amount_transferred`.

We need `a_i - f - amount_transferred ≤ x`, so `amount_transferred ≥ a_i - x - f`.

We also need `a_i - f - amount_transferred ≥ 0`, so `amount_transferred ≤ a_i - f`.

For this to be possible, we need `a_i - x - f ≤ a_i - f`, which is always true, and we need `a_i - x - f ≤ a_i - f`, which is true.

The critical constraint is `a_i - x - f ≤ a_i - f`, which is always true, and `a_i - x - f >= 0` is required for the existence of a valid transfer.

If `a_i - x - f < 0`, does that mean we can still do it?

If `a_i - x - f < 0`, then `a_i - x < f`, so `a_i - f < x`.

But we started with `a_i > x`, and after paying fee `f`, we have `a_i - f`.

We need `a_i - f ≤ x` for the source to have at most x.

If `a_i - f > x`, we need more transactions.

In our earlier derivation, we assumed one transaction is enough, but it's not always true.

For example: a_i = 10, x = 6, f = 5
- After one transaction: source becomes 10 - f - t = 5 - t
- We need 5 - t ≤ 6 (always true)
- But we also need the transferred amount to be non-negative
- We can choose t = 0, so source becomes 5
- Which is ≤ 6, so it works.

The constraint for the source to be valid is that `a_i - f - t ≤ x`, which is always true in this case since `a_i - f = 5 ≤ 6`.

Actually, `a_i - f - t` is at most `a_i - f`, and we want this to be ≤ x.

So we need `a_i - f ≤ x` for one transaction to be enough.

In the example: a_i = 10, x = 6, f = 5: 10-5 = 5 ≤ 6, so one transaction works.

What if a_i = 10, x = 5, f = 5?
- a_i - f = 5, x = 5, so 5 ≤ 5, still works.

What if a_i = 10, x = 4, f = 5?
- a_i - f = 5 > 4, so we need the source to have at most 4
- After transaction, source becomes 10 - 5 - t = 5 - t
- We need 5 - t ≤ 4, so t ≥ 1
- And 5 - t ≥ 0, so t ≤ 5
- So we can transfer t = 1, source becomes 4 ≤ 4
- So one transaction is enough.

What if a_i = 10, x = 3, f = 5?
- a_i - f = 5 > 3
- Source becomes 5 - t, need 5 - t ≤ 3, so t ≥ 2
- 5 - t ≥ 0, so t ≤ 5
- Transfer t = 2, source becomes 3, which is valid

So actually, for any a_i > x, we can always do it in one transaction by transferring enough to make the source ≤ x.

The only constraint is `a_i - x - f >= 0` for the amount transferred to be valid.

But actually, we don't need that. We can always transfer any amount up to a_i - f.

And we can choose to transfer `max(0, a_i - x - f)` or more.

The condition for the source to be ≤ x is `a_i - t - f ≤ x`, so `t ≥ a_i - x - f`.

If `a_i - x - f < 0`, then any non-negative t satisfies this (since t ≥ 0 > a_i - x - f).

So we can always transfer 0 if needed, but we need to ensure the source is ≤ x.

So the condition is that after the transaction, source ≤ x.

After transaction: `a_i - t - f ≤ x` → `t ≥ a_i - x - f`

If `a_i - x - f ≤ 0`, then t ≥ any negative number, so t=0 works.
If `a_i - x - f > 0`, then t ≥ a_i - x - f.

Also, we need `a_i - t - f ≥ 0` → `t ≤ a_i - f`

And we need `t ≤ x` (since we can't transfer more than x to a new address).

So t must satisfy: max(0, a_i - x - f) ≤ t ≤ min(x, a_i - f)

This is only possible if max(0, a_i - x - f) ≤ min(x, a_i - f)

Since a_i - x - f ≤ a_i - f, and a_i - x - f ≤ x only if a_i ≤ x + f + x = 2x + f.

But a_i > x, so we need to check when this is possible.

Actually, since a_i > x, a_i - x > 0.
We need max(0, a_i - x - f) ≤ min(x, a_i - f)

a_i - x - f ≤ a_i - f → true
a_i - x - f ≤ x → a_i ≤ 2x + f → always true since a_i ≤ 10^9, x,f ≤ 10^9

So the interval is non-empty.

Therefore, for any address with amount > x, we can always perform exactly one transaction to make it ≤ x.

So the total number of transactions is the number of addresses with amount > x.

Therefore, total fee = (number of addresses with amount > x) * f.

Let me verify with the example:
- Addresses: [13, 7, 6], x=6, f=2
- 13 > 6 → count = 1
- 7 > 6 → count = 2  
- 6 ≤ 6 → count = 2
- Total fee = 2 * 2 = 4 ✓

Perfect!

## Solution

```python
n = int(input())
addresses = list(map(int, input().split()))
x, f = map(int, input().split())

# Count number of addresses with amount > x
transactions = 0
for amount in addresses:
    if amount > x:
        transactions += 1

total_fee = transactions * f
print(total_fee)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

In the year 2xxx, an expedition team landing on a planet found strange objects made by an ancient species living on that planet. They are transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres (Figure 1). There are also many lithographs which seem to contain positions and radiuses of spheres.

<image>

Figure 1: A strange object

Initially their objective was unknown, but Professor Zambendorf found the cross section formed by a horizontal plane plays an important role. For example, the cross section of an object changes as in Figure 2 by sliding the plane from bottom to top.

<image>

Figure 2: Cross sections at different positions

He eventually found that some information is expressed by the transition of the number of connected figures in the cross section, where each connected figure is a union of discs intersecting or touching each other, and each disc is a cross section of the corresponding solid sphere. For instance, in Figure 2, whose geometry is described in the first sample dataset later, the number of connected figures changes as 0, 1, 2, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, and 0, at z = 0.0000, 162.0000, 167.0000, 173.0004, 185.0000, 191.9996, 198.0000, 203.0000, and 205.0000, respectively. By assigning 1 for increment and 0 for decrement, the transitions of this sequence can be expressed by an 8-bit binary number 11011000.

For helping further analysis, write a program to determine the transitions when sliding the horizontal plane from bottom (z = 0) to top (z = 36000).



Input

The input consists of a series of datasets. Each dataset begins with a line containing a positive integer, which indicates the number of spheres N in the dataset. It is followed by N lines describing the centers and radiuses of the spheres. Each of the N lines has four positive integers Xi, Yi, Zi, and Ri (i = 1, . . . , N) describing the center and the radius of the i-th sphere, respectively.

You may assume 1 ≤ N ≤ 100, 1 ≤ Ri ≤ 2000, 0 < Xi - Ri < Xi + Ri < 4000, 0 < Yi - Ri < Yi + Ri < 16000, and 0 < Zi - Ri < Zi + Ri < 36000. Each solid sphere is defined as the set of all points (x, y, z) satisfying (x - Xi)2 + (y - Yi)2 + (z - Zi)2 ≤ Ri2.

A sphere may contain other spheres. No two spheres are mutually tangent. Every Zi ± Ri and minimum/maximum z coordinates of a circle formed by the intersection of any two spheres differ from each other by at least 0.01.

The end of the input is indicated by a line with one zero.

Output

For each dataset, your program should output two lines. The first line should contain an integer M indicating the number of transitions. The second line should contain an M-bit binary number that expresses the transitions of the number of connected figures as specified above.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres
2. We're sliding a horizontal plane from bottom (z=0) to top (z=36000)
3. At each height z, we get a cross-section which is a union of circles (discs)
4. Connected figures are unions of discs that intersect or touch
5. We need to find when the number of connected components changes
6. For each increment (increase in connected components), we put '1'; for each decrement (decrease), we put '0'

## Key Insights

1. The number of connected components changes only at specific z-values
2. These z-values are the Z-coordinates where spheres enter or exit the plane
3. We need to find all critical z-values where events happen: sphere entering/exiting the plane
4. At each critical z-value, we compute the connected components and see if there's a change

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Identify Critical Z-values
For each sphere, the cross-section exists when z is between (Zi - Ri) and (Zi + Ri). So the critical z-values are:
- When z = Zi - Ri (sphere enters)
- When z = Zi + Ri (sphere exits)

### Step 2: At each critical z-value
- Find all circles (cross-sections of spheres) at that z-height
- Compute connected components of these circles
- Compare with previous state to detect transitions

### Step 3: Handle connected components
For circles at a given height z:
1. For each sphere, compute the circle in the xy-plane: center (Xi, Yi), radius = sqrt(Ri² - (z - Zi)²)
2. Two circles are connected if they intersect or touch
3. Use union-find or BFS/DFS to find connected components

### Step 4: Determine transitions
For each consecutive pair of state values (before and after critical z):
- If the number of components increases: append '1'
- If the number of components decreases: append '0'

```python
import math
from collections import defaultdict

def get_circle_radius(z, center_z, radius):
    """Calculate radius of circle at height z for a sphere with center at (x,y,z) and radius"""
    dz = abs(z - center_z)
    if dz >= radius:
        return 0  # sphere doesn't intersect the plane
    return math.sqrt(radius * radius - dz * dz)

def distance_sq(p1, p2):
    """Calculate squared distance between two points"""
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def get_connected_components(circles):
    """Find connected components of circles using union-find"""
    if not circles:
        return 0
    
    n = len(circles)
    if n == 1:
        return 1
    
    # Union-find data structure
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Two circles are connected if they intersect or touch
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # Check if circles i and j intersect or touch
            x1, y1, r1 = circles[i]
            x2, y2, r2 = circles[j]
            
            # Distance between centers
            dist_sq = distance_sq((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
            
            # Circles intersect or touch if distance <= r1 + r2
            if dist_sq <= (r1 + r2)**2:
                union(i, j)
    
    # Count number of unique roots
    components = len(set(find(i) for i in range(n)))
    return components

def solve_dataset(spheres):
    # Get all critical z-values
    critical_z = []
    for x, y, z, r in spheres:
        critical_z.append(z - r)  # sphere enters
        critical_z.append(z + r)  # sphere exits
    
    # Remove duplicates and sort
    critical_z = sorted(set(critical_z))
    
    # If no critical points, return empty string
    if not critical_z:
        return 0, ""
    
    # Process each critical z-value to find transitions
    transitions = []
    
    # Store state (number of connected components) at each critical z
    states = []
    
    # First add a dummy state to track changes
    states.append(get_connected_components([]))  # at z = -inf
    
    # Process each critical z-value
    for z in critical_z:
        # Get all circles at height z
        circles = []
        for x, y, z_c, r in spheres:
            dz = abs(z - z_c)
            if dz < r:  # sphere intersects the plane
                circle_radius = math.sqrt(r*r - dz*dz)
                circles.append((x, y, circle_radius))
        
        # Calculate number of connected components
        components = get_connected_components(circles)
        states.append(components)
    
    # Determine transitions between consecutive states
    result = ""
    for i in range(1, len(states)):
        if states[i] > states[i-1]:
            result += "1"
        else:
            result += "0"
    
    return len(result), result

# Main program
while True:
    n = int(input().strip())
    if n == 0:
        break
    
    spheres = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, y, z, r = map(int, input().split())
        spheres.append((x, y, z, r))
    
    m, binary = solve_dataset(spheres)
    print(m)
    print(binary)
```

import math
from collections import defaultdict

def get_circle_radius(z, center_z, radius):
    """Calculate radius of circle at height z for a sphere with center at (x,y,z) and radius"""
    dz = abs(z - center_z)
    if dz >= radius:
        return 0  # sphere doesn't intersect the plane
    return math.sqrt(radius * radius - dz * dz)

def distance_sq(p1, p2):
    """Calculate squared distance between two points"""
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def get_connected_components(circles):
    """Find connected components of circles using union-find"""
    if not circles:
        return 0
    
    n = len(circles)
    if n == 1:
        return 1
    
    # Union-find data structure
    parent = list(range(n))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    # Two circles are connected if they intersect or touch
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            # Check if circles i and j intersect or touch
            x1, y1, r1 = circles[i]
            x2, y2, r2 = circles[j]
            
            # Distance between centers
            dist_sq = distance_sq((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
            
            # Circles intersect or touch if distance <= r1 + r2
            if dist_sq <= (r1 + r2)**2:
                union(i, j)
    
    # Count number of unique roots
    components = len(set(find(i) for i in range(n)))
    return components

def solve_dataset(spheres):
    # Get all critical z-values
    critical_z = []
    for x, y, z, r in spheres:
        critical_z.append(z - r)  # sphere enters
        critical_z.append(z + r)  # sphere exits
    
    # Remove duplicates and sort
    critical_z = sorted(set(critical_z))
    
    # If no critical points, return empty string
    if not critical_z:
        return 0, ""
    
    # Process each critical z-value to find transitions
    transitions = []
    
    # Store state (number of connected components) at each critical z
    states = []
    
    # First add a dummy state to track changes
    states.append(get_connected_components([]))  # at z = -inf
    
    # Process each critical z-value
    for z in critical_z:
        # Get all circles at height z
        circles = []
        for x, y, z_c, r in spheres:
            dz = abs(z - z_c)
            if dz < r:  # sphere intersects the plane
                circle_radius = math.sqrt(r*r - dz*dz)
                circles.append((x, y, circle_radius))
        
        # Calculate number of connected components
        components = get_connected_components(circles)
        states.append(components)
    
    # Determine transitions between consecutive states
    result = ""
    for i in range(1, len(states)):
        if states[i] > states[i-1]:
            result += "1"
        else:
            result += "0"
    
    return len(result), result

# Main program
while True:
    n = int(input().strip())
    if n == 0:
        break
    
    spheres = []
    for _ in range(n):
        x, y, z, r = map(int, input().split())
        spheres.append((x, y, z, r))
    
    m, binary = solve_dataset(spheres)
    print(m)
    print(binary)

For helping further analysis, write a program to determine the transitions when sliding the horizontal plane from bottom (z = 0) to top (z = 36000).



Input

The input consists of a series of datasets. Each dataset begins with a line containing a positive integer, which indicates the number of spheres N in the dataset. It is followed by N lines describing the centers and radiuses of the spheres. Each of the N lines has four positive integers Xi, Yi, Zi, and Ri (i = 1, . . . , N) describing the center and the radius of the i-th sphere, respectively.

You may assume 1 ≤ N ≤ 100, 1 ≤ Ri ≤ 2000, 0 < Xi - Ri < Xi + Ri < 4000, 0 < Yi - Ri < Yi + Ri < 16000, and 0 < Zi - Ri < Zi + Ri < 36000. Each solid sphere is defined as the set of all points (x, y, z) satisfying (x - Xi)2 + (y - Yi)2 + (z - Zi)2 ≤ Ri2.

A sphere may contain other spheres. No two spheres are mutually tangent. Every Zi ± Ri and minimum/maximum z coordinates of a circle formed by the intersection of any two spheres differ from each other by at least 0.01.

The end of the input is indicated by a line with one zero.

For each dataset, your program should output two lines. The first line should contain an integer M indicating the number of transitions. The second line should contain an M-bit binary number that expresses the transitions of the number of connected figures as specified above.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have transparent boxes with opaque solid spheres inside
2. We're looking at horizontal cross-sections (at different z-levels)
3. Each cross-section shows circles (cross-sections of spheres) on a 2D plane
4. Connected figures are unions of circles that intersect or touch
5. We need to track how the number of connected components changes as z increases from 0 to 36000
6. Each transition (increment or decrement) is represented as 1 or 0 in binary

## Key Insights

1. At any z-level, each sphere creates a circle with radius √(R² - (z-Zi)²) and center (Xi, Yi)
2. The circle exists only when |z - Zi| ≤ Ri (sphere is cut by plane)
3. At the boundaries (z = Zi ± Ri), the circle appears/disappears
4. We need to find all critical z-values where the number of connected components changes
5. These critical points are exactly at z = Zi ± Ri for all spheres

## Algorithm Steps

1. Collect all critical z-values (Zi ± Ri) from all spheres
2. Sort these critical z-values
3. For each consecutive pair of critical z-values, determine if the number of connected components increases or decreases
4. Each increase = 1, each decrease = 0
5. Combine all these bits to form the final binary number

## Implementation Details

1. For each sphere, calculate the z-values where it starts and ends (Zi - Ri and Zi + Ri)
2. Create events for these z-values
3. For each interval between critical points, compute the connected components
4. Track the connected components by checking intersections between circles

## Circle Intersection Check

Two circles with centers (x1,y1) and (x2,y2) and radii r1 and r2 intersect if:
d² ≤ (r1 + r2)² where d is the distance between centers
and d² ≥ (r1 - r2)² (but since we need connected component formation, we just need to check if they touch or intersect)

Actually, for connected components, two circles are connected if they intersect or touch, so we need:
distance² ≤ (r1 + r2)²

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
import math
from collections import defaultdict

def distance_squared(p1, p2):
    return (p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2

def circles_intersect(c1, c2):
    x1, y1, r1 = c1
    x2, y2, r2 = c2
    d2 = distance_squared((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
    if d2 == 0:
        return r1 >= r2 or r2 >= r1
    # Circles intersect if distance^2 <= (r1 + r2)^2
    # and distance^2 >= (r1 - r2)^2 (they don't overlap completely, but just touch)
    return d2 <= (r1 + r2)**2

def get_connected_components(circles):
    if not circles:
        return 0
    
    # Use union-find or simply check connectivity
    n = len(circles)
    components = 0
    
    # Create adjacency matrix
    adj = [[False] * n for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            if circles_intersect(circles[i], circles[j]):
                adj[i][j] = adj[j][i] = True
    
    # Count connected components using DFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            components += 1
            # DFS to mark all connected nodes
            stack = [i]
            while stack:
                node = stack.pop()
                if visited[node]:
                    continue
                visited[node] = True
                for j in range(n):
                    if adj[node][j] and not visited[j]:
                        stack.append(j)
    
    return components

    # The transitions occur when crossing critical points

    critical_points = []
    for x, y, z, r in spheres:
        critical_points.append(z - r)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have transparent boxes with opaque solid spheres inside
2. We're sliding a horizontal plane from bottom (z=0) to top (z=36000)
3. At each z-level, we get a cross-section which is a collection of circles (cross-sections of spheres)
4. Connected figures are unions of circles that intersect or touch
5. We need to track when the number of connected components changes
6. For each transition (increment = 1, decrement = 0), we create a binary string

## Key Insights

1. The number of connected components changes only at specific z-values where circles either:
   - First touch each other (new connection)
   - Stop touching each other (disconnection)

2. Critical z-values are the z-coordinates where any two spheres' cross-sections touch or intersect
3. For two spheres with centers (x1,y1,z1,r1) and (x2,y2,z2,r2), their cross-sections touch when:
   - The distance between centers in xy-plane + z-distance ≤ r1 + r2
   - Specifically: √((x1-x2)² + (y1-y2)²) + |z1 - z2| = r1 + r2

4. At each critical z-value, we need to:
   - Determine the current number of connected components
   - Determine the next number of connected components
   - Record whether it increases (1) or decreases (0)

## Algorithm Steps

1. Generate all critical z-values where intersections occur
2. For each critical z-value, compute the current connected components
3. Determine if the count increases or decreases
4. Build the binary string from these transitions

## Implementation

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def distance_2d(p1, p2):
    return math.sqrt((p1[0] - p2[0])**2 + (p1[1] - p2[1])**2)

def get_critical_z_values(spheres):
    critical_z = set()
    n = len(spheres)
    
    # For each pair of spheres, find z-values where their cross-sections touch
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, z1, r1 = spheres[i]
            x2, y2, z2, r2 = spheres[j]
            
            # Distance in xy-plane
            d_xy = distance_2d((x1, y1), (x2, y2))
            
            # When do the cross-sections touch in z-direction?
            # The spheres' cross-sections touch when: d_xy + |z1 - z2| = r1 + r2
            # This means: |z1 - z2| = r1 + r2 - d_xy
            
            if d_xy <= r1 + r2:
                # The spheres can intersect
                z_diff = z2 - z1
                r_sum = r1 + r2
                
                # Two possible z-values for intersection
                # z1 + (r_sum - d_xy) and z2 + (r_sum - d_xy)
                # But we need to find where the horizontal cross-sections touch
                
                # The condition for intersection at height z is:
                # distance between centers at height z ≤ r1 + r2
                # (x1-x2)² + (y1-y2)² + (z-z1)² + (z-z2)² ≤ (r1+r2)²
                
                # Actually, for horizontal cross-section at height z:
                # The distance between (x1,y1,z) and (x2,y2,z) is √(d_xy² + (z-z1)² + (z-z2)²)
                # This equals the sum of radii when they touch
                
                # So: d_xy² + (z-z1)² + (z-z2)² = (r1+r2)²
                # (z-z1)² + (z-z2)² = (r1+r2)² - d_xy²
                
                # Expand: (z² - 2z*z1 + z1²) + (z² - 2z*z2 + z2²) = (r1+r2)² - d_xy²
                # 2z² - 2z(z1+z2) + (z1²+z2²) = (r1+r2)² - d_xy²
                
                # Solve quadratic: 2z² - 2(z1+z2)z + (z1²+z2² - (r1+r2)² + d_xy²) = 0
                
                a = 2.0
                b = -2.0 * (z1 + z2)
                c = z1*z1 + z2*z2 - (r1 + r2) * (r1 + r2) + d_xy * d_xy
                
                # Check if discriminant is non-negative
                if c >= 0:
                    discriminant = b * b - 4 * a * c
                    if discriminant >= 0:
                        sqrt_d = math.sqrt(discriminant)
                        z1_val = (-b + sqrt_d) / (2 * a)
                        z2_val = (-b - sqrt_d) / (2 * a)
                        critical_z.add(z1_val)
                        critical_z.add(z2_val)
    
    return sorted(critical_z)

def get_connected_components(spheres, z):
    # At height z, get the cross-section circles
    circles = []
    for x, y, z_center, r in spheres:
        # Circle at height z has radius sqrt(r² - (z - z_center)²)
        z_dist = abs(z - z_center)
        if z_dist <= r:
            circle_radius = math.sqrt(r * r - z_dist * z_dist)
            circles.append((x, y, circle_radius))
    
    # Use union-find or BFS to find connected components
    if not circles:
        return 0
    
    # For each circle, we have center (x,y) and radius
    # Two circles are connected if distance between centers <= sum of radii
    n = len(circles)
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    
    # Build graph where edges exist if circles touch or intersect
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            cx1, cy1, r1 = circles[i]
            cx2, cy2, r2 = circles[j]
            
            # Distance between centers
            dist = math.sqrt((cx1 - cx2) ** 2 + (cy1 - cy2) ** 2)
            
            # If distance <= r1 + r2, they are connected
            if dist <= r1 + r2:
                graph[i].append(j)
                graph[j].append(i)
    
    # Count connected components using DFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            components += 1
            # DFS
            stack = [i]
            while stack:
                node = stack.pop()
                if visited[node]:
                    continue
                visited[node] = True
                for neighbor in graph[node]:
                    if not visited[neighbor]:
                        stack.append(neighbor)
    
    return components

def solve():
    while True:
        n = int(input())
        if n == 0:
            break
            
        spheres = []
        for _ in range(n):
            x, y, z, r = map(int, input().split())
            spheres.append((x, y, z, r))
        
        # Get all critical z-values
        critical_z = get_critical_z_values(spheres)
        
        # Add boundaries (z=0 and z=36000)
        critical_z.extend([0.0, 36000.0])
        
        # Remove duplicates and sort
        critical_z = sorted(set(critical_z))
        
        # We need to check the transitions between consecutive critical points
        transitions = []
        
        for i in range(len(critical_z) - 1):
            z1 = critical_z[i]
            z2 = critical_z[i + 1]
            
            # Number of connected components at z1
            comp1 = get_connected_components(spheres, z1)
            
            # Number of connected components at z2
            comp2 = get_connected_components(spheres, z2)
            
            # Determine transition
            if comp2 > comp1:
                transitions.append('1')
            elif comp2 < comp1:
                transitions.append('0')
            else:
                # Same number of components, no transition
                transitions.append('0')
        
        # Remove trailing zeros (if any)
        # Actually, we need all transitions between critical points
        # The problem says "transitions when sliding from bottom to top"
        
        # Output
        print(len(transitions))
        if transitions:
            print(''.join(transitions))
        else:
            print('')
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

In the year 2xxx, an expedition team landing on a planet found strange objects made by an ancient species living on that planet. They are transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres (Figure 1). There are also many lithographs which seem to contain positions and radiuses of spheres.

<image>

Figure 1: A strange object

Initially their objective was unknown, but Professor Zambendorf found the cross section formed by a horizontal plane plays an important role. For example, the cross section of an object changes as in Figure 2 by sliding the plane from bottom to top.

<image>

Figure 2: Cross sections at different positions

He eventually found that some information is expressed by the transition of the number of connected figures in the cross section, where each connected figure is a union of discs intersecting or touching each other, and each disc is a cross section of the corresponding solid sphere. For instance, in Figure 2, whose geometry is described in the first sample dataset later, the number of connected figures changes as 0, 1, 2, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, and 0, at z = 0.0000, 162.0000, 167.0000, 173.0004, 185.0000, 191.9996, 198.0000, 203.0000, and 205.0000, respectively. By assigning 1 for increment and 0 for decrement, the transitions of this sequence can be expressed by an 8-bit binary number 11011000.

For helping further analysis, write a program to determine the transitions when sliding the horizontal plane from bottom (z = 0) to top (z = 36000).



Input

The input consists of a series of datasets. Each dataset begins with a line containing a positive integer, which indicates the number of spheres N in the dataset. It is followed by N lines describing the centers and radiuses of the spheres. Each of the N lines has four positive integers Xi, Yi, Zi, and Ri (i = 1, . . . , N) describing the center and the radius of the i-th sphere, respectively.

You may assume 1 ≤ N ≤ 100, 1 ≤ Ri ≤ 2000, 0 < Xi - Ri < Xi + Ri < 4000, 0 < Yi - Ri < Yi + Ri < 16000, and 0 < Zi - Ri < Zi + Ri < 36000. Each solid sphere is defined as the set of all points (x, y, z) satisfying (x - Xi)2 + (y - Yi)2 + (z - Zi)2 ≤ Ri2.

A sphere may contain other spheres. No two spheres are mutually tangent. Every Zi ± Ri and minimum/maximum z coordinates of a circle formed by the intersection of any two spheres differ from each other by at least 0.01.

The end of the input is indicated by a line with one zero.

Output

For each dataset, your program should output two lines. The first line should contain an integer M indicating the number of transitions. The second line should contain an M-bit binary number that expresses the transitions of the number of connected figures as specified above.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres
2. We're looking at cross-sections of these spheres at different z-levels (heights)
3. Each sphere creates a circle in the cross-section at height z
4. Connected figures are unions of circles that intersect or touch
5. We need to track transitions in the number of connected components as we move the plane from z=0 to z=36000
6. A transition is recorded as 1 for an increment (new connected component) and 0 for a decrement (component merges)

## Key Insights

1. As we move the horizontal plane from bottom to top, the cross-sections change
2. At certain z-values, circles appear, disappear, or merge
3. The critical points are where circles touch (intersect) or where they touch the plane
4. At each critical z-value, we calculate the number of connected components and record if it increases (1) or decreases (0)

## Solution Steps

1. Find all critical z-values where changes occur:
   - Where spheres first appear (z = Z_i - R_i)
   - Where spheres disappear (z = Z_i + R_i)
   - Where two spheres intersect (solving for intersection z)

2. For each critical z-value, determine the cross-section and count connected components

3. Track the transitions between consecutive z-values

## Algorithm

1. For each sphere, add the z-coordinates where it starts and ends
2. For each pairwise intersection, solve for z where two circles meet
3. Sort all critical z-values
4. For each consecutive pair of z-values, determine the connected components
5. Record the transition (increment or decrement)

```python
import math
from itertools import combinations

def get_circle_radius(x1, y1, z1, r1, x2, y2, z2, r2):
    """Calculate the radius of intersection between two circles at height z"""
    # Distance between centers in xy plane
    dx = x1 - x2
    dy = y1 - y2
    dist_xy = math.sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy)
    
    # The formula for intersection radius in z-direction
    # When two spheres intersect at height z, we solve:
    # (x-x1)^2 + (y-y1)^2 = r1^2 - (z-z1)^2
    # (x-x2)^2 + (y-y2)^2 = r2^2 - (z-z2)^2
    
    # The distance between centers in 3D space
    d = math.sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy + (z1-z2)*(z1-z2))
    
    # Check if they intersect
    if d >= (r1 + r2) or d <= abs(r1 - r2):
        return None
    
    # Calculate intersection z values using the formula
    # z1 + r1 >= z2 - r2 and z1 - r1 <= z2 + r2
    # The intersection occurs at z where the two circles meet
    
    # Calculate the height difference
    h = (r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d*d) / (2*d)
    
    # The distance from z1 to intersection point in z-direction
    h_z = math.sqrt(r1*r1 - h*h)
    
    # The intersection occurs at z = z1 + h_z and z = z1 - h_z
    # But we need to be careful about the geometry
    
    # Actually, let's determine the z-coordinates where the circles intersect
    # We solve for z such that the circles from two spheres touch
    
    # For spheres with centers (x1,y1,z1,r1) and (x2,y2,z2,r2)
    # The intersection points satisfy the system:
    # (x-x1)^2 + (y-y1)^2 + (z-z1)^2 = r1^2
    # (x-x2)^2 + (y-y2)^2 + (z-z2)^2 = r2^2
    
    # Subtracting the equations to eliminate x^2 + y^2:
    # (x-x1)^2 - (x-x2)^2 + (y-y1)^2 - (y-y2)^2 + (z-z1)^2 - (z-z2)^2 = r1^2 - r2^2
    
    # This gives us a linear equation in z
    # We can solve for z (but it's complex due to the quadratic nature)
    
    return None

def find_intersections(spheres):
    """Find all z-coordinates where two spheres intersect"""
    intersections = set()
    
    for i in range(len(spheres)):
        for j in range(i + 1, len(spheres)):
            x1, y1, z1, r1 = spheres[i]
            x2, y2, z2, r2 = spheres[j]
            
            # Distance between centers in 3D
            d3d = math.sqrt((x1-x2)**2 + (y1-y2)**2 + (z1-z2)**2)
            
            # Check if spheres intersect
            if d3d >= r1 + r2 or d3d <= abs(r1 - r2):
                continue
                
            # Find intersection points in z-direction
            # The circles in the cross-section intersect at certain z-values
            
            # The distance in xy-plane
            d_xy = math.sqrt((x1-x2)**2 + (y1-y2)**2)
            
            # If in xy-plane they are at distance d_xy, then the intersection
            # occurs when the circles in the horizontal plane touch
            
            # The z-coordinates of intersection points can be found by solving:
            # (z - z1)^2 + (r1^2 - (x1-x0)^2 - (y1-y0)^2) = 0
            
            # Instead of complex math, we'll use the fact that the cross-section circles
            # intersect when the distances satisfy certain conditions
            
            # We'll calculate the z-values where the circles touch
            
            # The formula for intersection z: 
            # z = z1 + (z2 - z1) * (some fraction)
            
            # Let's use the standard formula for intersection of two spheres
            # The intersection circle lies in a plane z = z_intersect
            
            # From sphere equations:
            # (x-x1)^2 + (y-y1)^2 + (z-z1)^2 = r1^2
            # (x-x2)^2 + (y-y2)^2 + (z-z2)^2 = r2^2
            
            # Expanding and subtracting:
            # (x1-x2)^2 + (y1-y2)^2 + (z1-z2)^2 + 2z(z2-z1) - 2z(z1-z2) = r1^2 - r2^2
            
            # Actually, let's simplify: the intersection occurs when the circles touch
            
            # The height constraint for intersection is when the distance in xy plane
            # plus z-distance equals the sum of radii
            
            # The intersection occurs at z such that the circles touch
            # This happens when the distance between centers equals the sum of radii
            
            # But they already satisfy that they intersect, so we need to find z
            
            # The correct approach is to solve for z in the plane of intersection
            # The intersection is in a plane z = h, and we solve for h
            
            # The distance from center1 to intersection plane
            # h = z1 + (z2 - z1) * (something)
            
            # Using the formula from sphere intersection:
            # h = z1 + (z2 - z1) * (r1^2 - r2^2 - d_xy^2) / (2*(r1^2 - r2^2 - d_xy^2 + d3d^2)) 
            # This is getting too complex
            
            # Alternative: since we know the spheres intersect, the cross-section
            # circles will have the same z-coordinate at intersection points
            
            # We'll calculate the z where the cross-sections intersect
            # We need to find z such that the points on the cross-sections coincide
            
            # The intersection happens when the circle from sphere1 at height z
            # and circle from sphere2 at height z have common points
            
            # For two circles in 2D at same height z:
            # (x-x1)^2 + (y-y1)^2 = r1^2 - (z-z1)^2
            # (x-x2)^2 + (y-y2)^2 = r2^2 - (z-z2)^2
            
            # The distance between centers in xy: d_xy
            # The difference in radii at height z: dr
            # They intersect when d_xy^2 <= (r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + (r2^2 - (z-z2)^2)
            
            # Actually, since they are 3D spheres, the intersection happens at specific z-values
            
            # We'll use the standard formula for intersection of two spheres
            # The intersection point lies in a plane z = h where:
            # h = z1 + (z2 - z1) * (r1^2 - r2^2 - d_xy^2) / (2 * (r1^2 - r2^2 - d_xy^2 + d3d^2)) 
            # This doesn't seem right
            
            # Let's use a different approach: find all critical z-values
            # where spheres start, end, or intersect
            
            # For intersection: the distance in xy is d_xy, and the distance in 3D is d3d
            # The intersection points occur at z such that the circles intersect
            
            # Using the formula: 
            # h = z1 + (z2 - z1) * (r1^2 - r2^2 - d_xy^2) / (2 * (d3d^2 - d_xy^2))
            # But this might not be correct
            
            # Actually, the intersection points exist in a plane parallel to xy-plane
            # The height of this plane is:
            # h = (r1^2 - r2^2 - d_xy^2 + d3d^2) / (2 * d3d)
            
            # Wait, let me recalculate: 
            # From the equation: (x-x1)^2 + (y-y1)^2 + (z-z1)^2 = r1^2
            # And (x-x2)^2 + (y-y2)^2 + (z-z2)^2 = r2^2
            # Subtract: (x1-x2)^2 + (y1-y2)^2 + (z1-z2)^2 + 2z(z2-z1) + (z1-z2)^2 = r1^2 - r2^2
            # This gives a linear equation in z?
            
            # Actually, when we expand and subtract:
            # (x1-x2)^2 + (y1-y2)^2 + (z1-z2)^2 + 2z(z2-z1) - 2z(z2-z1) + ... 
            # Let me expand properly:
            
            # (x-x1)^2 - (x-x2)^2 = (x1-x2)(x1-x2) - (x2-x2)(x2-x2) + 2x(x2-x1) + 2x(x1-x2)
            # This is messy
            
            # Standard formula: the intersection plane is at z = z1 + (z2-z1) * (r1^2 - r2^2 - d_xy^2) / (2 * (d3d^2 - d_xy^2))
            
            # I found the correct formula online: 
            # h = (r1^2 - r2^2 - d_xy^2 + d3d^2) / (2 * d3d)
            
            # Let me verify:
            # d3d^2 = d_xy^2 + (z1-z2)^2
            # So h = (r1^2 - r2^2 - d_xy^2 + d_xy^2 + (z1-z2)^2) / (2 * d3d) = (r1^2 - r2^2 + (z1-z2)^2) / (2 * d3d)
            
            # This is different from what I thought
            
            # Actually, from sphere intersection: the plane of intersection is at:
            # h = z1 + (z2 - z1) * (r1^2 - r2^2 - d_xy^2) / (2 * (r1^2 + r2^2 - d_xy^2 - (z1-z2)^2))
            # This seems wrong
            
            # I'll use a simpler approach: since we know the problem constraints, 
            # we can find critical points more straightforwardly
            
            # The key events are:
            # 1. When a sphere starts (z = z_i - r_i)
            # 2. When a sphere ends (z = z_i + r_i)
            # 3. When two spheres have circles that touch (intersect)
            
            # For intersection of circles in cross-section:
            # Two circles with centers (x1,y1) and (x2,y2) and radii r1(z) and r2(z)
            # intersect when distance between centers <= r1(z) + r2(z)
            
            # But for z where the circles touch, we solve:
            # d_xy^2 = (r1(z) + r2(z))^2
            # where r1(z) = sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) and r2(z) = sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2)
            
            # This gives: d_xy^2 = (sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2))^2
            # This is a complicated equation to solve
            
            # Given the problem complexity and the constraint on precision, 
            # I'll use a numerical approach to find intersection points
            
            # The intersection z values are where the circles touch
            # We'll find roots of the equation f(z) = 0 where d_xy^2 = (r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + (r2^2 - (z-z2)^2) - 2*sqrt((r1^2 - (z-z1)^2)(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2))
            
            # But instead, since we only need the events, we can use the known fact:
            # The intersection points occur at z where the circles touch
            
            # I'll use the formula from standard intersection:
            # The intersection plane is at height z_int = (r1^2 - r2^2 + d_xy^2 + (z1-z2)^2) / (2 * d3d)
            
            # This gives a single z value where the intersection occurs
            
            # Calculate the intersection height
            numerator = r1*r1 - r2*r2 + d_xy*d_xy + (z1-z2)*(z1-z2)
            denominator = 2 * d3d
            
            if abs(denominator) < 1e-6:
                continue
                
            z_int = numerator / denominator
            
            # Add intersection point if it's within valid range
            if (z1 - r1 < z_int < z1 + r1) and (z2 - r2 < z_int < z2 + r2):
                intersections.add(z_int)
    
    return intersections

def get_cross_section_components(spheres, z):
    """Get the number of connected components in cross-section at height z"""
    circles = []
    
    for x, y, z_center, r in spheres:
        # Check if sphere reaches this height
        if z_center - r <= z <= z_center + r:
            # Calculate radius of circle at height z
            h = z - z_center
            radius_sq = r*r - h*h
            if radius_sq < 0:
                continue
            radius = math.sqrt(radius_sq)
            
            circles.append((x, y, radius))
    
    # Count connected components
    if not circles:
        return 0
    
    # Use union-find or flood fill to count connected components
    # Since circles are small, we'll use a simple approach
    
    # Sort by x-coordinate, then y-coordinate
    circles.sort()
    
    # For each circle, check if it intersects with any previous circle
    components = 1
    # Use a list to track which circles are connected to which
    connected_to = [i for i in range(len(circles))]
    
    # Check pairwise intersection
    for i in range(len(circles)):
        for j in range(i + 1, len(circles)):
            x1, y1, r1 = circles[i]
            x2, y2, r2 = circles[j]
            
            # Distance between centers
            dx = x1 - x2
            dy = y1 - y2
            dist = math.sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy)
            
            # If distance <= r1 + r2, circles intersect or touch
            if dist <= r1 + r2:
                # Mark that they are connected
                # We'll use a BFS-like approach
                # For simplicity, we'll count components using a different method
                
                # Instead of tracking connections, we'll use a simpler approach:
                # If two circles intersect, they belong to the same component
                
                # But this is complex to track in general
                
                # For the purpose of this problem, let's use a simple check
                # and assume that if two circles are close enough, they're connected
                pass
    
    # Simpler approach: use the fact that the problem is about transitions
    # We can use a standard algorithm for circle connectivity
    
    # Create a graph and find connected components
    n = len(circles)
    if n == 0:
        return 0
    
    # Create adjacency list
    graph = [[] for _ in range(n)]
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, r1 = circles[i]
            x2, y2, r2 = circles[j]
            dx = x1 - x2
            dy = y1 - y2
            dist = math.sqrt(dx*dx + dy*dy)
            
            if dist <= r1 + r2:
                graph[i].append(j)
                graph[j].append(i)
    
    # Count connected components using DFS
    visited = [False] * n
    components = 0
    
    for i in range(n):
        if not visited[i]:
            components += 1
            # DFS
            stack = [i]
            while stack:
                node = stack.pop()
                if not visited[node]:
                    visited[node] = True
                    for neighbor in graph[node]:
                        if not visited[neighbor]:
                            stack.append(neighbor)
    
    return components

def solve_dataset(spheres):
    if not spheres:
        return 0, "0"
    
    # Find all critical z-values
    critical_z = []
    
    # Add start and end points of each sphere
    for x, y, z_center, r in spheres:
        start_z = z_center - r
        end_z = z_center + r
        critical_z.append(start_z)
        critical_z.append(end_z)
    
    # Add intersection points between spheres
    intersections = find_intersections(spheres)
    for z in intersections:
        critical_z.append(z)
    
    # Remove duplicates and sort
    critical_z = sorted(set(critical_z))
    
    # Add boundaries
    critical_z = [0.0] + critical_z + [36000.0]
    
    # Track transitions
    transitions = []
    
    # Go through consecutive critical points
    for i in range(len(critical_z) - 1):
        z1 = critical_z[i]
        z2 = critical_z[i + 1]
        
        # Calculate number of components at z1 and z2
        components_before = get_cross_section_components(spheres, z1)
        components_after = get_cross_section_components(spheres, z2)
        
        # Check if transition occurs
        if components_after > components_before:
            transitions.append('1')
        elif components_after < components_before:
            transitions.append('0')
        # If equal, no transition
    
    # Remove transitions at boundaries if they don't represent actual changes
    # The problem states transitions as the change in number of connected figures
    # So only when the count changes
    
    # Remove non-changes
    changed_transitions = []
    for i in range(len(transitions)):
        if i == 0:
            # First transition
            changed_transitions.append(transitions[i])
        else:
            # Only if it's different from previous
            if transitions[i] != transitions[i-1]:
                changed_transitions.append(transitions[i])
    
    # Actually, the transitions should be between consecutive critical points
    # But we need to detect changes in component count between consecutive z-values
    
    # Let's recalculate: we only care about changes in component count between z1 and z2
    transitions = []
    for i in range(len(critical_z) - 1):
        z1 = critical_z[i]
        z2 = critical_z[i + 1]
        
        # Calculate components at z1 and z2
        comp1 = get_cross_section_components(spheres, z1)
        comp2 = get_cross_section_components(spheres, z2)
        
        if comp2 > comp1:
            transitions.append('1')
        elif comp2 < comp1:
            transitions.append('0')
    
    # Convert to string
    result = ''.join(transitions)
    return len(transitions), result

def main():
    while True:
        n = int(input())
        if n == 0:
            break
            
        spheres = []
        for _ in range(n):
            x, y, z, r = map(int, input().split())
            spheres.append((x, y, z, r))
        
        # Solve the dataset
        m, binary = solve_dataset(spheres)
        print(m)
        print(binary)

if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

In the year 2xxx, an expedition team landing on a planet found strange objects made by an ancient species living on that planet. They are transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres (Figure 1). There are also many lithographs which seem to contain positions and radiuses of spheres.

<image>

Figure 1: A strange object

Initially their objective was unknown, but Professor Zambendorf found the cross section formed by a horizontal plane plays an important role. For example, the cross section of an object changes as in Figure 2 by sliding the plane from bottom to top.

<image>

Figure 2: Cross sections at different positions

He eventually found that some information is expressed by the transition of the number of connected figures in the cross section, where each connected figure is a union of discs intersecting or touching each other, and each disc is a cross section of the corresponding solid sphere. For instance, in Figure 2, whose geometry is described in the first sample dataset later, the number of connected figures changes as 0, 1, 2, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, and 0, at z = 0.0000, 162.0000, 167.0000, 173.0004, 185.0000, 191.9996, 198.0000, 203.0000, and 205.0000, respectively. By assigning 1 for increment and 0 for decrement, the transitions of this sequence can be expressed by an 8-bit binary number 11011000.

For helping further analysis, write a program to determine the transitions when sliding the horizontal plane from bottom (z = 0) to top (z = 36000).



Input

The input consists of a series of datasets. Each dataset begins with a line containing a positive integer, which indicates the number of spheres N in the dataset. It is followed by N lines describing the centers and radiuses of the spheres. Each of the N lines has four positive integers Xi, Yi, Zi, and Ri (i = 1, . . . , N) describing the center and the radius of the i-th sphere, respectively.

You may assume 1 ≤ N ≤ 100, 1 ≤ Ri ≤ 2000, 0 < Xi - Ri < Xi + Ri < 4000, 0 < Yi - Ri < Yi + Ri < 16000, and 0 < Zi - Ri < Zi + Ri < 36000. Each solid sphere is defined as the set of all points (x, y, z) satisfying (x - Xi)2 + (y - Yi)2 + (z - Zi)2 ≤ Ri2.

A sphere may contain other spheres. No two spheres are mutually tangent. Every Zi ± Ri and minimum/maximum z coordinates of a circle formed by the intersection of any two spheres differ from each other by at least 0.01.

The end of the input is indicated by a line with one zero.

Output

For each dataset, your program should output two lines. The first line should contain an integer M indicating the number of transitions. The second line should contain an M-bit binary number that expresses the transitions of the number of connected figures as specified above.

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres
2. We're analyzing cross sections created by horizontal planes at different z-positions
3. Each sphere creates a circular cross section at height z, with radius determined by the distance from z to the sphere's center
4. Connected figures are unions of circles that touch or intersect
5. We need to track how the number of connected components changes as z increases from 0 to 36000
6. For each transition (increment/decrement in connected components), we record 1 for increment, 0 for decrement
7. Output the count of transitions and the binary string

## Key Insights

1. At height z, a sphere with center (Xi, Yi, Zi) and radius Ri contributes a circle of radius √(Ri² - (z - Zi)²) if |z - Zi| ≤ Ri
2. The circle exists only when z is within [Zi - Ri, Zi + Ri]
3. When circles overlap or touch, they form connected components
4. We need to detect when connected components change - this happens at critical z-values where circles start/end or touch each other
5. At these critical points, we calculate if the number of connected components increases or decreases

## Step-by-Step Solution

1. **Find critical z-values**: These are the z-coordinate boundaries where circles appear/disappear or touch
2. **For each critical z-value**: Determine the number of connected components in the cross section
3. **Track transitions**: Between consecutive critical z-values, count if the number of components increases or decreases
4. **Generate binary string**: 1 for increase, 0 for decrease

## Implementation

```python
import math
from collections import defaultdict

def get_circle_radius(sphere, z):
    """Get radius of circle at height z for sphere (x, y, z_center, r)"""
    dz = abs(z - sphere[2])
    if dz > sphere[3]:
        return 0
    return math.sqrt(sphere[3] * sphere[3] - dz * dz)

def get_circle_intersection_points(circles):
    """Get z-values where circles intersect or touch"""
    intersections = set()
    
    for i in range(len(circles)):
        for j in range(i + 1, len(circles)):
            circ1, circ2 = circles[i], circles[j]
            
            # Two circles at same height z
            # Circle 1: center (x1, y1), radius r1
            # Circle 2: center (x2, y2), radius r2
            dx = abs(circ1[0] - circ2[0])
            dy = abs(circ1[1] - circ2[1])
            r1, r2 = circ1[2], circ2[2]
            
            # If circles touch or intersect
            if dx <= r1 + r2 and dx >= abs(r1 - r2):
                # If they're exactly touching
                if dx == r1 + r2 or dx == abs(r1 - r2):
                    # They touch at a point - this happens at current z
                    # The intersection point in x-y plane, but z is fixed
                    # We only care about the z value, which is current z
                    intersections.add(circ1[3])
                    
                    # Check if they overlap for this z
                    if dx < r1 + r2 and dx > abs(r1 - r2):
                        # They intersect - this is always at current z
                        intersections.add(circ1[3])
    
    return intersections

def get_connected_components(circles):
    """Get number of connected components formed by circles"""
    if not circles:
        return 0
    
    # Group circles by connections
    # Two circles are connected if their distance between centers <= sum of radii
    connected = defaultdict(list)
    
    for i in range(len(circles)):
        for j in range(len(circles)):
            if i != j:
                dx = abs(circles[i][0] - circles[j][0])
                dy = abs(circles[i][1] - circles[j][1])
                dist = math.sqrt(dx * dx + dy * dy)
                r1, r2 = circles[i][2], circles[j][2]
                if dist <= r1 + r2:  # They touch or intersect
                    # Connect circles i and j
                    connected[i].append(j)
                    connected[j].append(i)
    
    # Use union-find to count connected components
    parent = list(range(len(circles)))
    
    def find(x):
        if parent[x] != x:
            parent[x] = find(parent[x])
        return parent[x]
    
    def union(x, y):
        px, py = find(x), find(y)
        if px != py:
            parent[px] = py
    
    for i in range(len(circles)):
        for j in range(len(circles)):
            if i != j and dist <= r1 + r2:
                union(i, j)
    
    # Count unique roots
    roots = set()
    for i in range(len(circles)):
        roots.add(find(i))
    
    return len(roots)

def solve():
    while True:
        n = int(input().strip())
        if n == 0:
            break
            
        spheres = []
        for _ in range(n):
            x, y, z, r = map(int, input().split())

        critical_z = set()

        for x, y, z, r in spheres:
            critical_z.add(z - r)
            critical_z.add(z + r)
        
        # Add intersection points - for each pair of spheres, find if they intersect at any height
        for i in range(n):

                x1, y1, z1, r1 = spheres[i]
                x2, y2, z2, r2 = spheres[j]
                
                # Distance between centers in xy plane
                d_xy = math.sqrt((x1 - x2) ** 2 + (y1 - y2) ** 2)
                
                # For intersection to happen, we need d_xy <= r1 + r2
                if d_xy <= r1 + r2:
                    # The z-coordinates where the circles intersect
                    # We need to find z where the circles touch
                    # The distance between centers is fixed, so we can solve for z
                    
                    # The minimum distance between spheres in z-direction

                    # Actually, we need to solve for intersection of two circles
                    # This is complex - simpler approach: if they intersect, add boundary points
                    
                    # We'll consider all combinations of sphere boundaries
                    pass
        
        # More straightforward: collect all z-values where components change
        # These are sphere boundaries and intersection points
        
        # Convert to sorted list
        critical_z = list(critical_z)
        critical_z.sort()
        
        # Also need to consider when circles touch or intersect
        # For each pair of spheres, find z where they touch
        
        # For overlapping circles, they touch when the distance between centers equals sum of radii
        # For spheres with centers (x1,y1,z1,r1) and (x2,y2,z2,r2)
        # The distance between centers is sqrt((x1-x2)^2 + (y1-y2)^2 + (z1-z2)^2)
        # For intersection, this distance must be <= r1 + r2
        # The circles at height z have distances:
        # d_xy = sqrt((x1-x2)^2 + (y1-y2)^2)
        # d_z = |z - z1| + |z - z2|  - no, this is not right

        # Circle 1: radius = sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2)
        # Circle 2: radius = sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2)
        # They touch when distance between centers in xy = sum of radii

        # They touch when d_xy = sqrt(r1^2 - (z-z1)^2) + sqrt(r2^2 - (z-z2)^2)
        
        # This equation is transcendental and hard to solve analytically
        # But since we only care about whether components change, we can use a different approach

        # 1. When a sphere starts or stops contributing (at z = z_i ± r_i)
        # 2. When two circles that were separate become connected (intersect)
        # 3. When two circles that were connected become separate (stop intersecting)

        # and when the circles intersect.
        
        # For now, let's use a simpler approach:
        
        # All critical points are sphere boundaries
        critical_z = list(critical_z)
        critical_z.sort()

        # For each pair of spheres, if they intersect, add z-values where they touch
        # This is complex to compute exactly, so let's use an approximation

        # So let's collect all z-values from sphere boundaries (z_i ± r_i)
        # And then between them, determine the connected components

            critical_points.add(z - r)

        # Since the differences are at least 0.01, we can step through these values

        events = []
        
        for x, y, z, r in spheres:
            events.append((z - r, 1))  # circle starts
            events.append((z + r, -1)) # circle ends
        
        events.sort()
        
        # Now simulate the process by going through events
        # Keep track of connected components at each interval
        
        # For each interval between events, compute connected components
        transitions = []
        
        # The connected components change only at events (start/end of circles)
        # But they might also change when two circles that touch each other come into contact
        
        # To be safe, we'll evaluate at each critical point, and between them
        # The number of components changes at events where the addition/removal of a circle
        # causes a change in connectivity
        
        # Let's simulate step by step
        z_values = []
        
        # First add all events

        z_values.sort()

        # and track transitions

        def get_circles_at_z(z):
            circles = []
            for x, y, z_center, r in spheres:
                if abs(z - z_center) <= r:
                    radius = math.sqrt(r * r - (z - z_center) * (z - z_center))
                    circles.append((x, y, radius))
            return circles

        components_at_z = []
        
        # We need to evaluate at each critical point
        for z in z_values:
            circles = get_circles_at_z(z)
            # Compute connected components
            if len(circles) == 0:
                components_at_z.append(0)
            else:
                # Use union-find to find connected components
                parent = list(range(len(circles)))
                
                def find(x):
                    if parent[x] != x:
                        parent[x] = find(parent[x])
                    return parent[x]
                
                def union(x, y):
                    px, py = find(x), find(y)
                    if px != py:
                        parent[px] = py
                
                # Check all pairs of circles
                for i in range(len(circles)):

                        if dist <= r1 + r2:
                            union(i, j)

                roots = set()

                    roots.add(find(i))
                components_at_z.append(len(roots))

        transitions = []
        for i in range(1, len(components_at_z)):
            if components_at_z[i] > components_at_z[i-1]:

        critical_z = sorted(set(critical_z))

                components_at_z.append(0)
```

Problem

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

In the year 2xxx, an expedition team landing on a planet found strange objects made by an ancient species living on that planet. They are transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres (Figure 1). There are also many lithographs which seem to contain positions and radiuses of spheres.

<image>

Figure 1: A strange object

Initially their objective was unknown, but Professor Zambendorf found the cross section formed by a horizontal plane plays an important role. For example, the cross section of an object changes as in Figure 2 by sliding the plane from bottom to top.

<image>

Figure 2: Cross sections at different positions

He eventually found that some information is expressed by the transition of the number of connected figures in the cross section, where each connected figure is a union of discs intersecting or touching each other, and each disc is a cross section of the corresponding solid sphere. For instance, in Figure 2, whose geometry is described in the first sample dataset later, the number of connected figures changes as 0, 1, 2, 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, and 0, at z = 0.0000, 162.0000, 167.0000, 173.0004, 185.0000, 191.9996, 198.0000, 203.0000, and 205.0000, respectively. By assigning 1 for increment and 0 for decrement, the transitions of this sequence can be expressed by an 8-bit binary number 11011000.

For helping further analysis, write a program to determine the transitions when sliding the horizontal plane from bottom (z = 0) to top (z = 36000).



Input

The input consists of a series of datasets. Each dataset begins with a line containing a positive integer, which indicates the number of spheres N in the dataset. It is followed by N lines describing the centers and radiuses of the spheres. Each of the N lines has four positive integers Xi, Yi, Zi, and Ri (i = 1, . . . , N) describing the center and the radius of the i-th sphere, respectively.

You may assume 1 ≤ N ≤ 100, 1 ≤ Ri ≤ 2000, 0 < Xi - Ri < Xi + Ri < 4000, 0 < Yi - Ri < Yi + Ri < 16000, and 0 < Zi - Ri < Zi + Ri < 36000. Each solid sphere is defined as the set of all points (x, y, z) satisfying (x - Xi)2 + (y - Yi)2 + (z - Zi)2 ≤ Ri2.

A sphere may contain other spheres. No two spheres are mutually tangent. Every Zi ± Ri and minimum/maximum z coordinates of a circle formed by the intersection of any two spheres differ from each other by at least 0.01.

The end of the input is indicated by a line with one zero.

Output

For each dataset, your program should output two lines. The first line should contain an integer M indicating the number of transitions. The second line should contain an M-bit binary number that expresses the transitions of the number of connected figures as specified above.

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have transparent boxes containing opaque solid spheres
2. We're looking at cross-sections created by horizontal planes at different z levels
3. Each sphere creates a circular cross-section at height z
4. Connected figures are unions of circles that intersect or touch
5. We need to find when the number of connected components changes as we move the plane from z=0 to z=36000
6. We output the number of transitions and a binary number indicating whether each transition is an increment (1) or decrement (0)

## Key Insights

1. At any z-level, each sphere contributes a circle with radius r = √(R² - (z-Zi)²) if |z-Zi| ≤ Ri
2. When the plane moves, the radius of circles changes, and connections between circles can form or break
3. Transitions occur when circles touch or when a circle appears/disappears
4. We need to find all critical z-values where the number of connected components changes

## Algorithm Steps

1. Find all critical z-values where something significant happens:
   - When a sphere first appears (z = Zi - Ri)
   - When a sphere disappears (z = Zi + Ri)
   - When two circles touch (z = Zi ± Ri ± √(Ri² - (distance_between_centers_in_xy)²))

2. For each critical z-value, calculate the current number of connected components
3. Compare consecutive states to determine if it's an increment (1) or decrement (0)

## Implementation Details

1. For each pair of spheres, calculate when their cross-section circles touch
2. Sort all critical z-values and evaluate the state at each critical point
3. Track the changes between consecutive states

```python
import math
from collections import deque

def distance_squared_2d(x1, y1, x2, y2):
    return (x1 - x2) ** 2 + (y1 - y2) ** 2

def get_distance_2d(x1, y1, x2, y2):
    return math.sqrt((x1 - x2) ** 2 + (y1 - y2) ** 2)

def solve_dataset(spheres):
    # All critical z-values
    z_events = set()
    
    # Add when spheres appear/disappear
    for x, y, z, r in spheres:
        z_events.add(z - r)
        z_events.add(z + r)
    
    # Add when circles of two spheres touch (in cross-section)
    n = len(spheres)
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n):
            x1, y1, z1, r1 = spheres[i]
            x2, y2, z2, r2 = spheres[j]
            
            # Distance between centers in xy-plane
            d_xy = get_distance_2d(x1, y1, x2, y2)
            
            # For circles to intersect in cross-section, we need:
            # |r1 - r2| < d_xy < r1 + r2
            # The touching occurs when the distance between centers = r1 + r2 or |r1 - r2|
            
            # For external touch: d_xy = r1 + r2
            if d_xy <= r1 + r2:
                # The circles touch at z such that the distance in z-direction satisfies

        if not circles:
            states.append(0)
            continue
```
